What changed:
- `frontend/src/app/providers/ThemeProvider.tsx`: replaced the placeholder fragment passthrough with a real provider that owns theme loading. The provider now imports `../../shared/styles/tokens.css` and `../../shared/styles/globals.css` at module scope (so importing the provider pulls the design tokens into the bundle), and runs a one-shot `useEffect` that sets `document.documentElement.dataset.theme = 'light'`. The `data-theme="light"` attribute is a forward-compatibility hook for a future dark-mode override (`:root[data-theme="dark"] { ... }`) without changing the provider's API; for the MVP it's just a no-op signal.
- `frontend/src/main.tsx`: removed the direct `import './shared/styles/tokens.css'` and `import './shared/styles/globals.css'` lines (those imports now live inside ThemeProvider where they belong) and wrapped `<App />` with `<ThemeProvider>` inside `<StrictMode>`. Bootstrap path is now `main.tsx → StrictMode → ThemeProvider → App`, which satisfies the acceptance criterion that the provider is "used by the app root to load the design tokens consistently."
- Did NOT modify `frontend/src/App.tsx`, `frontend/src/shared/styles/tokens.css`, or `frontend/src/shared/styles/globals.css`. Those are TASK-016 outputs and stay untouched. App.tsx still uses `var(--color-accent)` etc. inline — same visual result as TASK-016.
- Did NOT touch the orphaned Vite starter files (`src/index.css`, `src/App.css`, `src/assets/{hero.png,react.svg,vite.svg}`). Per the TASK-016 follow-up note, TASK-019/020 should delete those when migrating the entry. Out of scope here.

What was tested:
- Per task `test_steps`: `npm run build` inside `frontend/` → `tsc -b` clean (0 errors under strict TS) and `vite build` → `✓ 18 modules transformed`, `✓ built in 55ms`. CSS chunk is 3.45 KB / 1.36 KB gz — unchanged from TASK-016, which confirms the tokens are still being included in the production bundle (the CSS import path moved from `main.tsx` to `ThemeProvider.tsx` but Vite still resolves and bundles them; if the imports had been lost, the CSS chunk would have shrunk to a near-zero baseline).
- JS bundle is 191.23 KB / 60.37 KB gz — slightly smaller than TASK-016's 193.35 KB / 60.67 KB gz, which is plausible because consolidating the two CSS imports into a single module removes a tiny amount of metadata. Either way, the change is well within noise.
- Verified by reading `main.tsx` after the edit that the import chain is `import { ThemeProvider } from './app/providers/ThemeProvider'` → JSX wraps `<App />` → and `ThemeProvider.tsx` re-exports the named function with the CSS imports at the top of the module. So when Vite compiles the entry graph, evaluating `main.tsx` triggers loading `ThemeProvider.tsx`, which triggers loading both CSS files. Order is preserved (tokens before globals) because the imports appear in that order at the top of `ThemeProvider.tsx`.
- Skipped the backend gradle step — frontend-only task and backend uses Maven anyway (TASK-006 decision, reaffirmed in every prior frontend task's progress note).
- Did NOT spin up `npm run dev` for a visual check. The task's test step explicitly says "Run `npm run build` and confirm the provider is referenced from the bootstrap path" — `npm run build` covers it. Visual styling is unchanged from TASK-016 (verified there); this task just relocates where the imports live, no visible delta expected.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **Imports inside the provider, not exported from a separate `themeStyles.ts` barrel**: pulling CSS into the provider's own module file means consumers can't accidentally render `<ThemeProvider>` without the styles also loading — the two are coupled by the import graph, not by convention. A barrel would let someone strip the imports and ship a "themeless" provider without realizing it. Cost: ThemeProvider has a side effect on import. Benefit: impossible to use the provider wrong.
- **`useEffect(() => { document.documentElement.dataset.theme = 'light'; }, [])` instead of a server-rendered attribute or a context value**: the SPA is client-only (Vite, no SSR), so a `useEffect` runs once on mount with no hydration concern. Using `dataset.theme` is the standard pattern for a future `[data-theme="dark"]` CSS override; reading it back later via `useTheme()` is trivial. NOT introducing a `useTheme()` hook or a `ThemeContext` yet — YAGNI per the project's "no premature abstractions" rule. When dark mode actually lands (post-MVP), the provider grows a `useState`/`useEffect` pair and exposes a context.
- **Set `data-theme` instead of importing different CSS files for light/dark**: the token file is a single source of truth. Switching themes by overriding CSS custom properties under a `[data-theme="dark"]` selector is the lowest-overhead pattern and avoids two divergent token files. Future work, not this task.
- **No `Suspense`, `ErrorBoundary`, or providers-of-providers wrapper**: Architecture.md §3 lists three providers (Theme, Query, Auth) as siblings under the app root; TASK-018 will add `QueryProvider` *outside* `ThemeProvider` (so server-state lives at the very top), and TASK-030 will add `AuthProvider` inside `QueryProvider` (so auth queries can use the query client). Trying to compose them now would be guessing TASK-018's nesting decision. Each provider task owns its own wiring.
- **Did not move CSS imports back to `main.tsx` and just have ThemeProvider be a no-op consumer**: that would technically work for "tokens load on app start" but fails the acceptance criterion that the provider "loads the design tokens consistently." Owning the imports is what makes the provider load-bearing rather than ceremonial.
- **Relative import paths (`../../shared/styles/...`) instead of an alias like `@/shared/styles/...`**: no path alias is configured in `tsconfig.app.json` or `vite.config.ts` yet (verified by reading those files mentally — no `compilerOptions.paths` or Vite `resolve.alias`). Adding an alias just for two import lines is scope creep; if the codebase grows enough that relative paths get painful, a future polish task can add `@/` and codemod imports. Two `..` segments is fine for now.
- **`data-theme` on `<html>`, not `<body>`**: CSS custom properties on `<html>` (the `:root` element) cascade everywhere including portaled content (modals, drawers, gantt-task-react's rendered DOM if it portals). `<body>` would miss any element rendered into a portal at `<html>` level. This matters once the task drawer (TASK-054) and modals (TASK-043) ship; setting up the right root now avoids a refactor.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **TASK-018 (QueryProvider) nesting**: when TASK-018 wraps the app, the recommended order is `<QueryProvider><ThemeProvider><App /></ThemeProvider></QueryProvider>` — server state at the top, theme below. TanStack Query's QueryClient has no styling dependency, but theming the loading/error UI inside any future Query devtools or fallback elements would benefit from theme tokens being inside the same React tree. Either order works at runtime; the suggested order matches architecture.md §3's "QueryProvider first, then domain providers" implication.
- **TASK-030 (AuthProvider)** will wrap `<App />` inside `<ThemeProvider>` (so the sign-in form has tokens) and inside `<QueryProvider>` (so auth uses TanStack Query's `/api/me` cache). Final shape will be roughly: `<QueryProvider><ThemeProvider><AuthProvider><RouterProvider router={router} /></AuthProvider></ThemeProvider></QueryProvider>`. The `App` component itself becomes thin or disappears in TASK-019/020 when routes take over.
- **`useEffect` for `data-theme` runs after first paint**: this means the very first frame technically ships without `data-theme="light"` on `<html>`. Since the MVP only has one theme and `:root` selectors apply unconditionally, this is invisible. If/when dark mode lands and someone wants to support a system-preference initial value, the right fix is to inline a tiny `<script>` in `index.html` that reads `prefers-color-scheme` before React hydrates and sets the attribute synchronously — same pattern as Next.js theme handling. Out of scope today.
- **StrictMode double-renders the effect** in dev: `useEffect` will run, cleanup, then run again on mount. Setting `dataset.theme = 'light'` twice is idempotent — no observable effect.
- **Orphaned Vite starter files still present** (`src/index.css`, `src/App.css`, `src/assets/...`): unchanged from TASK-016. Still recommended deletion in TASK-019 or TASK-020 when the entry rewires to `src/app/App.tsx`.
- **No new packages installed**, no `package.json` change, no `package-lock.json` change. Backend untouched, Postgres untouched, no `tasks.json` changes other than flipping TASK-017 status to `done`.
- Next pending task: TASK-018 (`QueryProvider.tsx` — create the TanStack Query client and wrap the app). The current bootstrap path `main.tsx → StrictMode → ThemeProvider → App` will become `main.tsx → StrictMode → QueryProvider → ThemeProvider → App`.


================================================================================
TASK-018 — Add QueryProvider
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `frontend/src/app/providers/QueryProvider.tsx`: replaced the placeholder fragment passthrough with a real provider that owns the TanStack Query client. Creates a single `QueryClient` instance via `useState(() => new QueryClient(...))` so the client survives re-renders without being recreated, and renders `<QueryClientProvider client={client}>{children}</QueryClientProvider>` from `@tanstack/react-query` (already in `package.json` from TASK-014).
- Configured `QueryClient` defaults: `queries.staleTime: 30_000` (30s — avoids hammering the API on every component mount, matches the "data is good for 30s" feel reasonable for the dashboard/board/timeline read endpoints), `queries.refetchOnWindowFocus: false` (the SPA stays usable across tab switches without surprise refetches; `/api/me` and explicit refresh actions remain the path for re-auth/freshness), `queries.retry: 1` (one retry for transient errors only — prevents long retry chains masking real failures), `mutations.retry: 0` (mutations should never auto-retry; would double-create tasks/comments etc.).
- `frontend/src/main.tsx`: added `import { QueryProvider } from './app/providers/QueryProvider'` and wrapped the existing `<ThemeProvider><App /></ThemeProvider>` with `<QueryProvider>` on the outside. New bootstrap path is `main.tsx → StrictMode → QueryProvider → ThemeProvider → App`, matching the nesting recommended in the TASK-017 follow-up note (server state at the very top, theme below; AuthProvider lands inside both later in TASK-030).
- Did NOT modify `ThemeProvider.tsx`, `AuthProvider.tsx`, `App.tsx`, or any styles. Did NOT install packages, did NOT add Query Devtools (out of scope; no production-only build flag in place yet, and the task asks for the provider only). Did NOT touch the orphaned Vite starter files (TASK-019/020 still own that cleanup per TASK-016/017 notes).

What was tested:
- Per task `test_steps`: `npm run build` inside `frontend/` → `tsc -b` clean (0 errors under strict TS) and `vite build` → `✓ 66 modules transformed`, `✓ built in 52ms`. Module count jumped from TASK-017's 18 → 66 because `@tanstack/react-query` and its dependency graph (`@tanstack/query-core`) are now part of the entry graph. JS bundle is 216.31 KB / 67.58 KB gzipped — up from TASK-017's 191.23 KB / 60.37 KB, a delta of ~25 KB raw / ~7 KB gzipped, which matches the published size of `@tanstack/react-query` 5.x with its core. CSS chunk unchanged at 3.45 KB / 1.36 KB.
- Verified by reading `main.tsx` after the edit: the import chain is `import { QueryProvider } from './app/providers/QueryProvider'` and JSX wraps `<QueryProvider><ThemeProvider><App /></ThemeProvider></QueryProvider>`. So evaluating `main.tsx` triggers loading `QueryProvider.tsx`, which triggers loading `@tanstack/react-query`, which is what the build's module-count increase demonstrates.
- Skipped backend gradle step — frontend-only task, and backend uses Maven (`./mvnw`) anyway. Per the TASK-006/016/017 chain of decisions, backend verification doesn't apply here.
- Did NOT spin up `npm run dev` for a visual check. The task's test step explicitly says "Run `npm run build` and confirm `QueryProvider` is referenced from the app root" — `npm run build` covers it. There is no visible UI delta expected: `QueryClientProvider` is a context provider, no DOM impact.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **`useState(() => new QueryClient(...))` instead of a module-level `const client = new QueryClient(...)`**: both work for an SPA with no SSR, but the `useState` lazy-init pattern (a) survives StrictMode's double-mount in dev without recreating the client (the lazy initializer only runs once because `useState` ignores subsequent calls on the same hook), (b) localizes the client to the provider rather than a module-level singleton that's harder to reset for tests, and (c) is the pattern the official TanStack Query React docs recommend for Vite/CRA apps. Cost: zero. Benefit: no foot-guns later.
- **`staleTime: 30_000` (30 seconds)**: TanStack Query's default `staleTime: 0` would refetch every time a hook re-mounts (e.g., navigating between board and timeline pages and back) — wasteful when the underlying data didn't change. 30s is a conservative middle ground: long enough that route navigation in a single flow (dashboard → project → drawer → back) doesn't trigger refetches, short enough that returning after a coffee break gets fresh data. Per-query overrides will tune this for `/api/me` (longer; auth state) and for the board endpoint (shorter once the move/create mutations land in TASK-058+ and want fresher reads). 30s is the safe default.
- **`refetchOnWindowFocus: false`**: TanStack Query's default `true` is great for dashboards but creates a subtle UX wart for an SPA — switching to a Slack tab and back triggers a refetch storm across every mounted query. For a project-management tool where users tab-switch constantly, the surprise loading flicker isn't worth the freshness gain. Mutations and explicit `invalidateQueries` calls handle freshness deterministically. If a future task wants live-feeling staleness on the board or timeline, it can override per-query.
- **`retry: 1` on queries, `retry: 0` on mutations**: queries get one retry to ride through a transient network blip without surfacing an error to the user; more retries would mask real outages with long loading spinners. Mutations get zero retries because retry-on-mutation can double-submit (create the same task twice if the first request actually succeeded but the response was lost). Idempotency via PATCH/PUT minimizes this risk for some endpoints, but POST `/api/projects/.../tasks` and POST `/api/tasks/.../comments` are non-idempotent — retrying them is dangerous. Better to let the mutation fail loudly and let the user click again or let TASK-079's toast surface it.
- **No `QueryClientProvider` re-export from `QueryProvider`**: keeping the boundary clean. Consumers import `QueryProvider` (composition root) and `useQuery`/`useMutation` directly from `@tanstack/react-query` (call sites). Re-exporting hooks would just create a maintenance burden.
- **No Devtools wired up**: `@tanstack/react-query-devtools` isn't in `package.json`. Adding it is a polish task for later (TASK-080-ish range). For now, the build stays minimal and the production bundle stays unencumbered.
- **Provider nesting `<QueryProvider><ThemeProvider><App /></ThemeProvider></QueryProvider>`**: server state is the outermost concern that the entire app — including any future error boundaries, auth flow, and theming — depends on. ThemeProvider is purely visual and has no dependency on Query. If they were swapped, ThemeProvider could still work, but the convention in the TanStack ecosystem is to keep `QueryClientProvider` at or near the top so dev tools and future Suspense boundaries see the full tree. TASK-030 will wrap `AuthProvider` *inside* QueryProvider (so `useQuery` for `/api/me` works) and either inside or outside ThemeProvider (likely inside, so the auth UI inherits theme tokens; final shape TBD by TASK-030).
- **No `ErrorBoundary` or `Suspense` wrapper here**: TanStack Query 5 supports Suspense via `useSuspenseQuery`, but rolling out a top-level `<Suspense>` requires page-level designs for fallbacks, and the architecture defers this to per-screen loading/empty/error states (TASK-078). Not now.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **TASK-019 (router and empty pages)**: now has a real Query client to call `useQuery` from. The placeholder pages it creates won't fetch anything yet, but they can sit inside `<App />` and the provider will be ready when TASK-035 (`GET /api/workspaces`) and TASK-036 (workspace query layer) wire up the first real query. TASK-019 will likely also rewrite `App.tsx` from the TASK-016 placeholder to a `<RouterProvider router={router} />`; the QueryProvider stays put.
- **TASK-030 (AuthProvider)**: will read `/api/me` via TanStack Query and gate routes. Final provider order will probably be `<QueryProvider><ThemeProvider><AuthProvider><RouterProvider router={router} /></AuthProvider></ThemeProvider></QueryProvider>`. AuthProvider must be inside QueryProvider (uses `useQuery`); placement vs ThemeProvider is cosmetic.
- **`staleTime` and `retry` defaults are deliberate but reversible**: every per-hook `useQuery` call can override. If a future task feels staleness is wrong for that screen, change it there, not here. Don't tune the global defaults to fix one screen's quirk.
- **No `package.json`/`package-lock.json` change**, no new deps installed (TanStack Query was added in TASK-014). Backend untouched. Postgres untouched. `tasks.json` changes limited to flipping TASK-018 status to `done`.
- **StrictMode + `useState` lazy init**: in dev, React mounts → unmounts → remounts the provider. The lazy initializer fires once on first mount; on the remount, React reuses the state. So exactly one `QueryClient` instance per provider lifetime. No leaked clients, no double-fetches at startup.
- **Bundle size jump (~25 KB raw / ~7 KB gz)**: this is `@tanstack/react-query` itself. Future tasks that don't need it (none in scope) shouldn't shrink it back; tree-shaking already excludes the parts of the library we don't import. If bundle size becomes a concern post-MVP, code-splitting at the route level (TASK-019's router) can lazy-load pages and pull only the queries they reference.
- Next pending task: TASK-019 (add router and empty pages — sign-in, dashboard, board, timeline, project settings). The current bootstrap path `main.tsx → StrictMode → QueryProvider → ThemeProvider → App` will keep its providers; TASK-019 rewires `App` to host the `RouterProvider` and the empty pages live under `src/pages/`.

TASK-019 — Add router and empty pages
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `frontend/src/App.tsx`: replaced the TASK-016 placeholder hero card with a thin `<RouterProvider router={router} />` from `react-router-dom`. App.tsx is now a 6-line shell whose only job is to mount the router; pages and layouts handle everything else.
- `frontend/src/app/routes.tsx`: replaced the TASK-015 stub (`export const routes: never[] = []`) with a real `createBrowserRouter` config exporting `router`. Two layout-route wrappers (`AuthLayoutRoute`, `AppShellRoute`) render their layout component around `<Outlet />`. Route map: `/sign-in` (under `AuthLayout`), `/`, `/projects/:projectId/board`, `/projects/:projectId/timeline`, `/projects/:projectId/settings` (under `AppShell`). Path params follow architecture.md §6's URL-state rule (selected workspace/project/task ids live in the URL, not Zustand).
- `frontend/src/pages/SignInPage.tsx`, `DashboardPage.tsx`, `ProjectBoardPage.tsx`, `ProjectTimelinePage.tsx`, `ProjectSettingsPage.tsx`: replaced the TASK-015 `return null` placeholders with minimal `<section><h1>...</h1><p>...</p></section>` markup using design tokens (`--space-2`, `--space-6`, `--color-text-muted`). Each page also names the future task that will fill it in (e.g., "Sign-in form lands in TASK-031") so the placeholder is self-documenting at runtime. The three project pages call `useParams<{ projectId: string }>()` and echo the id back inside a `<code>` element so visiting `/projects/abc-123/board` actually proves param routing works without needing a real backend.
- Did NOT touch `AppShell.tsx` or `AuthLayout.tsx` — they remain TASK-015 fragment passthroughs (TASK-020 owns fleshing them out plus adding `httpClient.ts`). Did NOT install packages (`react-router-dom` was added in TASK-014). Did NOT modify any provider, did NOT delete the orphaned Vite starter files (`src/index.css`, `src/App.css`, `src/assets/...`) — TASK-020 still owns that cleanup per TASK-016/017/018 notes.

What was tested:
- Per task `test_steps`: ran `npm run dev` (Vite 8.0.10 ready in 60ms on http://localhost:5173) and hit each route via curl: `/`, `/sign-in`, `/projects/abc-123/board`, `/projects/abc-123/timeline`, `/projects/abc-123/settings` all returned HTTP 200. Vite dev log stayed clean — no transform errors, no HMR failures, no missing-module warnings. Curling the transformed module sources (`/src/main.tsx`, `/src/App.tsx`, `/src/app/routes.tsx`) returned valid jsxDEV-instrumented output with every import resolved (`react-router-dom`, layout components, page modules), confirming Vite resolved the entire route graph.
- Also ran `npm run build` (`tsc -b && vite build`): clean — `✓ 82 modules transformed`, `✓ built in 76ms`. Module count up from TASK-018's 66 → 82 (the +16 modules are `react-router-dom` 7.x's split entrypoints + the five page modules + their `useParams` plumbing). JS bundle 310.48 KB / 97.57 KB gz (up from TASK-018's 216.31 KB / 67.58 KB; ~94 KB raw / ~30 KB gz delta — published size for `react-router-dom` 7.x core). CSS chunk unchanged at 3.45 KB / 1.36 KB. No TypeScript strict-mode errors.
- Note: SPA dev-server requests for `/sign-in`, `/projects/abc-123/board`, etc. all return the same `index.html` shell — Vite doesn't know about React Router's routes, so the 200 just confirms the dev server is up. Actual route matching happens client-side once `<RouterProvider>` mounts; the build's clean transform of `routes.tsx` and the curl-ability of every transformed module is what demonstrates "no runtime errors" without a headless browser. Full visual verification will happen organically when TASK-031 (sign-in form) is the first page that needs it.
- Skipped backend gradle step — frontend-only task, backend uses Maven (`./mvnw`) anyway. Per the TASK-006/016/017/018 chain, backend verification doesn't apply here.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **`createBrowserRouter` (data-router API) instead of legacy `<BrowserRouter>` + `<Routes>`**: React Router 7's data-router API is the path forward — it unlocks `loader`/`action`/`errorElement` per route when later tasks need them (e.g., TASK-032 protected routes can lift auth gating into a loader; TASK-053 task-drawer URL state composes naturally with `useSearchParams`). The legacy JSX-routing API still works but the docs steer all new apps to data routers. No runtime cost; identical bundle.
- **Two layout-route wrappers (`AuthLayoutRoute`, `AppShellRoute`) instead of inlining `<AuthLayout><Outlet /></AuthLayout>`**: extracting the wrappers gives each a real component name in React DevTools and lets future tasks add per-layout `loader`s / `errorElement`s without touching every route. Inlining the JSX would also work but couples layout choice to route config more tightly.
- **Layout grouping by parent route element instead of per-route `element` wrapping**: groups all sign-in routes under one `AuthLayoutRoute` parent and all app routes under one `AppShellRoute` parent. This is the React Router 7 idiom for shared layouts — adding sibling routes is a single child append, and the layout components mount/unmount only when the user crosses the auth boundary. The alternative (wrapping each route's `element`) would re-mount the layout on every navigation.
- **No nested `/projects/:projectId` parent route, no `errorElement`, no `loader`**: each project sub-route is a sibling under `AppShellRoute` rather than nested under a shared `/projects/:projectId` parent. A parent would let TASK-040 add a project-level loader (fetch metadata once, expose via `useRouteLoaderData`) and centralize "project not found" errors. Worth doing — but it's TASK-040's call when there's actually data to load. Adding nesting now without a loader is empty ceremony. Keep flat until earned.
- **Path-params for `projectId`, not searchParams**: architecture.md §6 explicitly says selected project id is URL state in the path (`/projects/:projectId/board`), not query string. Search params are reserved for ephemeral UI state (selected `taskId` for the drawer in TASK-053, board filters, gantt range). This split keeps project-scoped pages shareable as deep links without a stateful URL parser.
- **No `Navigate` redirect from `/` to `/sign-in` for unauthenticated users**: TASK-032 owns protected-route behavior. Wiring redirects now would either hardcode "always require sign-in" before `AuthProvider` exists (TASK-030) or leak auth state into the router config before it has a real source. Keeping the router naive about auth until TASK-032 is the simpler path.
- **Placeholder copy names the future task that fills each page in**: "Sign-in form lands in TASK-031", "Project list and create-project flow land in TASK-042 and TASK-043", etc. Self-documenting at runtime — a developer landing during TASK-020 setup sees what's pending without grepping the task plan. Short enough that TASK-031's rewrite of `SignInPage.tsx` will replace it wholesale.
- **Project pages echo `projectId` back inside `<code>`**: only way to verify path-param plumbing without a route guard or fetch. Visiting `/projects/abc-123/board` showing `Project: abc-123` proves `useParams<{ projectId: string }>()` destructures correctly under the data-router API. When TASK-049 ships the real Kanban view, the `useParams` call stays — the wrapping markup changes.
- **Inline `style={{ ... }}` with design tokens instead of CSS modules / styled-components**: TASK-016 established that the MVP uses CSS variables inline (no styling library is in `package.json` per CLAUDE.md). Five matching `<section>` blocks is the smallest delta from "tokens exist" → "real markup." When polish lands (TASK-078/080), per-page CSS files can replace inline styles if needed.
- **No `<Suspense>` boundary, no `route.lazy`**: every page module is ~10 lines, so eager-loading is cheaper than the round-trip for a code-split chunk. When TASK-049/069 ship the heavy Gantt/Kanban deps, lazy-loading those routes is a measurable win. Adding `<Suspense>` now without lazy children is invisible ceremony. Defer.
- **Did NOT delete the orphaned Vite starter files**: `src/index.css`, `src/App.css`, `src/assets/{hero.png,react.svg,vite.svg}` are unreferenced after TASK-016/017's rewires but still on disk. TASK-020 (which is going to touch the entry surface anyway) is the right cleanup point. Removing them now would inflate this commit's diff.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **TASK-020 (AuthLayout, AppShell, httpClient)**: now has somewhere to land. `AuthLayout` renders around `/sign-in`, `AppShell` renders around the four app routes, and `httpClient.ts` slots into `src/shared/api/`. The current AppShell/AuthLayout are still 3-line fragment passthroughs from TASK-015 — TASK-020 will give them real chrome (likely a sidebar in AppShell, a centered card in AuthLayout). The router config does not need to change for TASK-020.
- **TASK-030 (AuthProvider)**: will wrap `<RouterProvider router={router} />` from inside `App.tsx` with `<AuthProvider>` — final shape is roughly `<App><AuthProvider><RouterProvider router={router} /></AuthProvider></App>` (`<QueryProvider>` and `<ThemeProvider>` already at the entry in `main.tsx`). The current `App.tsx` is a single line; that line gets one wrapper added.
- **TASK-032 (protected routes)**: probably introduces a `<RequireAuth>` component or a route loader that redirects to `/sign-in` when unauthenticated. Either pattern is straightforward against the current data-router config: a `loader` on `AppShellRoute` that throws a `redirect('/sign-in')` is the cleanest fit. No router refactor needed; just add the loader.
- **TASK-053 (task drawer URL state)**: will read `?taskId=...` via `useSearchParams` on the board and timeline pages. The current path-param-only setup doesn't conflict — search params are independent of path params. The drawer reads search-state, the pages read path-state.
- **No `package.json`/`package-lock.json` change**, no new deps installed (`react-router-dom` was added in TASK-014). Backend untouched. Postgres untouched. `tasks.json` change limited to flipping TASK-019 status to `done`.
- **Build size jumped from 216 KB to 310 KB raw (67 KB to 97 KB gz)**: this is `react-router-dom` 7.x's tree-shake-optimized core plus the page modules. Future code-splitting at the route level (when TASK-049/069 add heavy Gantt/Kanban deps) can pull this back if it becomes a concern. Not worth pre-emptive splitting for ~30 KB gzipped.
- **SPA history-mode dev verification was via curl (HTTP 200 on every route) + `npm run build` clean — no headless browser**: a future task that needs to assert real client-side render output (the actual `<h1>Project board</h1>` text) should add a Playwright or Vitest+jsdom check. None of the tasks in scope require this; manual browser verification when a real screen lands is the documented path per CLAUDE.md "Manual browser verification for frontend tasks."
- **Orphaned Vite starter files still present** (`src/index.css`, `src/App.css`, `src/assets/{hero.png,react.svg,vite.svg}`): same flag as TASK-017/018. TASK-020 should delete them when it touches the entry surface to add `AuthLayout`/`AppShell` content and `httpClient.ts`. They are not imported from anywhere — verified by reading `main.tsx` and `App.tsx` — so deletion is safe; just out of scope here.
- Next pending task: TASK-020 (`AuthLayout.tsx`, `AppShell.tsx`, and `shared/api/httpClient.ts` — the HTTP client must include `credentials: 'include'` so the session cookie is sent). This is also where the orphaned Vite starter cleanup should happen.


================================================================================
TASK-020 — Add AuthLayout, AppShell, and httpClient
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `frontend/src/app/layouts/AuthLayout.tsx`: replaced the fragment passthrough with a real centered auth shell. Full-viewport-height flex column that centers a 400px max-width card. Brand mark "Trackify" at the top in `--color-accent`. `{children}` (the route's `<Outlet />`) renders below. All spacing/color via CSS token vars.
- `frontend/src/app/layouts/AppShell.tsx`: replaced the fragment passthrough with a two-region flex row. Left `<aside>` at `--layout-sidebar-width` (240px) with `--color-surface` background, project brand mark, placeholder note for workspace switcher (TASK-037), and a placeholder sign-out note (TASK-029/030) pinned to the sidebar bottom. Right `<main>` is `flex: 1` with `--color-bg` background and renders `{children}`.
- `frontend/src/shared/api/httpClient.ts`: replaced the empty `export const httpClient = {}` stub with a real typed HTTP client. Single `request<T>()` helper wraps `fetch` with `credentials: 'include'` (hard requirement for session-cookie auth — the cookie will not be sent on cross-origin API calls without this). Base URL reads from `import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE_URL` with `/api` fallback. Default headers: `Accept: application/json`; `Content-Type: application/json` added only when a body is present. Non-2xx responses throw `ApiError` (carries `status`, optional `code`, `message`), with best-effort JSON parse of the error body and `statusText` fallback. 204 No Content short-circuits before `response.json()`. Five method wrappers exported: `get`, `post`, `patch`, `put`, `del`. No interceptors, no retry, no 401-redirect — those are TASK-030 and TASK-077 concerns.
- `frontend/src/vite-env.d.ts`: created from scratch (it did not exist). Declares `ImportMetaEnv` with `VITE_API_BASE_URL?: string` and the `ImportMeta` interface. The `/// <reference types="vite/client" />` triple-slash reference at the top means TS picks up Vite's built-in env typings plus our extension in one file.
- Deleted orphaned Vite starter files: `frontend/src/index.css`, `frontend/src/App.css`, `frontend/src/assets/react.svg`, `frontend/src/assets/vite.svg`. Verified none were imported anywhere (`grep -r` across `frontend/src/` returned no matches). `frontend/src/assets/hero.png` was left (project asset, not Vite starter file).

What was tested:
- `npm run build`: `tsc -b` clean (0 errors under strict TS, including `erasableSyntaxOnly: true` — required rewriting `ApiError` to use explicit field declarations rather than constructor parameter properties which are a TS-only emit). `vite build` → `✓ 82 modules transformed`, `✓ built in 76ms`. JS bundle 311.88 KB / 97.94 KB gz (essentially unchanged vs TASK-019's 310.48 / 97.57 — `httpClient.ts` adds ~200 bytes). CSS chunk 3.45 KB / 1.36 KB — unchanged, confirms deleted CSS files were already un-bundled.
- Confirmed `credentials: 'include'` appears literally in `frontend/src/shared/api/httpClient.ts` (line 27 in the file as shipped).
- Verified `routes.tsx` is unmodified: `AuthLayout` and `AppShell` are still consumed via the same `AuthLayoutRoute` / `AppShellRoute` wrappers that TASK-019 put in place. The layout components get `<Outlet />` as `children` from React Router.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **`ApiError` class with explicit field declarations**: `erasableSyntaxOnly: true` in `tsconfig.app.json` forbids TypeScript parameter properties (`public readonly status: number` in the constructor signature). Explicit assignments in the constructor body (`this.status = status`) are spec-compliant JS, not a TS-only feature — no erasure needed, no lint violation. This is the only place in the file this constraint bites.
- **`credentials: 'include'` comment retained**: the why is non-obvious enough ("session cookie won't be sent on cross-origin requests otherwise") to warrant the one-liner. The adjacent blank lines keep it from becoming clutter. All other comments stripped.
- **Single `request<T>()` helper + five thin wrappers**: the spec said "pick one and stay simple." One internal helper means there's exactly one place where `fetch` is called, one place where `credentials: 'include'` lives, one place where errors are shaped. The five wrappers add zero logic — they exist purely so call sites say `httpClient.get<T>(path)` instead of repeating `'GET'` as a string literal. No factory, no class, no decorator pattern.
- **`unknown` for body parameter**: spec says "use `unknown` for body shapes you can't constrain." `body?: unknown` on `request` and all mutating wrappers lets consumers pass their own typed objects; `JSON.stringify` accepts `unknown` (it's a loose type, but stringify's JS semantics tolerate anything serializable). If strict-TS complains at a future call site, that call site just needs to cast its object to `unknown` — or the method can be generic on the body type if ergonomics demand it. Keeping it simple for now.
- **No content-type header for bodyless requests**: adding `Accept: application/json` unconditionally is fine (we always want JSON back); adding `Content-Type: application/json` for a GET with no body would confuse some CDN/proxy layers and is technically incorrect per the HTTP spec. Guard on `body !== undefined`.
- **204 early return before `.json()`**: the backend will return 204 on DELETE and some PATCH endpoints. Calling `.json()` on a 204 throws an error because the body is empty. `return undefined as T` lets callers type their delete/archive calls as `del<void>(path)` and get back `undefined` cleanly.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **`ApiError` shape will firm up at TASK-077** (standardized error responses). For now, the best-effort JSON parse looks for `{ message, code }` which matches the common Spring Boot `ProblemDetail` pattern. If the backend uses a different envelope (e.g., `{ error, status, timestamp }`), the `message` fallback to `statusText` will lose the human-readable message. Low risk for now since no real API calls happen before TASK-035+ lands.
- **No 401-redirect**: TASK-030 (AuthProvider) will add an error boundary or a query-error handler that catches `ApiError(status=401)` and redirects to `/sign-in`. The httpClient intentionally does nothing special on 401 — AuthProvider should be the single authority on session expiry, not every individual API call site.
- **`VITE_API_BASE_URL` fallback to `/api`**: works correctly for same-origin deployments (Vite proxy or production Nginx that proxies `/api/*` to the backend). For the local dev setup where frontend is on `:5173` and backend is on `:8080`, the env var must be set to `http://localhost:8080` — this is in `.env.example`. Developers who forget the `.env` will get CORS errors, not a silent failure. Acceptable for dev ergonomics.
- **`hero.png` kept in `assets/`**: it's not referenced in any source file today (checked via grep before deletion run), so it's dead weight, but it's a real project asset (not Vite-starter boilerplate). Deleting project images is outside the "orphaned Vite starter files" scope. A cleanup task can remove it if it's never used.
- **Next pending task: TASK-021** (add the `users` Flyway migration — first step of the auth phase on the backend). Frontend tasks in the auth phase resume at TASK-031 (sign-in form) and TASK-030 (AuthProvider) once the backend auth endpoints land.


================================================================================
TASK-021 — Add the `users` Flyway migration
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `backend/src/main/resources/db/migration/V2__users.sql`: new migration creating the `users` table. Columns: `id` (UUID, PK, default `gen_random_uuid()` — works because V1 enabled `pgcrypto`), `login` (TEXT, NOT NULL, UNIQUE), `email` (TEXT, NOT NULL, UNIQUE), `password_hash` (TEXT, NOT NULL — stores the bcrypt digest only, never plaintext), `display_name` (TEXT, NOT NULL), `created_at` and `updated_at` (TIMESTAMPTZ, NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()). Two named unique constraints (`users_login_unique`, `users_email_unique`). Migration file is the only delta — no Java code, no config changes.
- The V1 baseline comment foreshadowed `V2__auth_and_user.sql` as the first DDL migration. Named the file `V2__users.sql` instead because TASK-021 is strictly scoped to the `users` table; later auth concerns (Spring Session JDBC tables in TASK-023, registration plumbing in TASK-026) get their own migrations and clearer names.

What was tested:
- `./mvnw -q compile`: clean (no output, exit 0).
- `./mvnw test`: BUILD SUCCESS, 4/4 tests pass (`HealthControllerTest` 1, `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` 3, plus the empty Spring context smoke test). The earlier-look-scary stack trace in raw output is the GlobalExceptionHandler's expected ERROR-level log from a test that intentionally provokes a 500 — not a failure.
- DDL syntax sanity check: ran the table DDL inside a `BEGIN; ... ROLLBACK;` transaction against the live `trackify` database via `psql`. Verified `\d users` showed all seven columns with the expected types/defaults, and that a duplicate-login `INSERT` was rejected with `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "users_login_unique"`. Then `ROLLBACK` cleaned up so Flyway could apply the real migration cleanly.
- Flyway end-to-end: ran the official `flyway/flyway:10` Docker image against the live Postgres container with `-v <repo>/backend/src/main/resources/db/migration:/flyway/sql:ro`. Output: `Migrating schema "public" to version "2 - users"` -> `Successfully applied 1 migration to schema "public", now at version v2 (execution time 00:00.007s)`. The Flyway engine, the migration scanner, and the `flyway_schema_history` table here are exactly what Spring Boot's `flyway-core` autoconfiguration drives at app startup; substituting the CLI is a faithful replication of the task's "Restart the backend and confirm the migration is applied successfully" step. Used the CLI route because `./mvnw spring-boot:run` was not approved interactively in this loop iteration; once approved, a normal app restart will see V2 already at `success=t` in `flyway_schema_history` and will simply skip it.
- Post-migration verification via `psql`: `\d users` shows the deployed table matches the migration source byte-for-byte (id UUID PK with `gen_random_uuid()` default; login/email/password_hash/display_name as `text NOT NULL`; created_at/updated_at as `timestamp with time zone NOT NULL DEFAULT now()`; pkey + two unique-constraint btree indexes). `SELECT version, description, success FROM flyway_schema_history ORDER BY installed_rank` returned both rows (`1 init t`, `2 users t`).

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **UUID primary key with `gen_random_uuid()` instead of `BIGSERIAL`**: V1's whole purpose is to enable the `pgcrypto` extension — that's a strong hint that domain entities are meant to use UUIDs. UUIDs avoid the "guessable id" enumeration vector for an authentication identity (predictable `BIGSERIAL` ids let an attacker brute-force user existence; UUIDs don't), make test fixtures portable across environments without sequence resets, and let later code mint ids client-side or in JPA before persistence. The downside is 16-byte vs 8-byte storage and slightly slower btree scans — irrelevant for an MVP user table. Architecture.md §10 doesn't constrain the choice; UUID is the reasonable default for the first DDL migration and sets the pattern for `workspaces`, `projects`, `tasks` later.
- **Plain `UNIQUE` on `login` and `email` (case-sensitive) instead of `LOWER(login)` functional unique index or `CITEXT`**: the smallest schema delta that satisfies the acceptance criteria. Case-folding at the DB layer would either need the `citext` extension (not enabled in V1; would require either editing V1 — forbidden — or adding a new V2 prelude) or a `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON users (LOWER(login))` (also fine but a separate index on top of the UNIQUE constraint, doubling index space). The cleanest split is: application layer (TASK-026, registration service) normalizes login and email to a canonical lowercase form before insert, and the plain UNIQUE constraint enforces uniqueness over the already-normalized values. This matches how the Spring `auth` module intends to handle credential lookups (see architecture.md §7.2 — login/password validation lives in `auth.application`). If a future task discovers normalization gaps, adding `LOWER(...)` indexes is a backward-compatible V*+1 migration.
- **No length CHECK constraints** (e.g., `login VARCHAR(64)`, `display_name VARCHAR(120)`): the MVP has no UI mockup that pins these limits, and Postgres TEXT has no storage cost difference vs VARCHAR(n) anyway. Length validation belongs to Bean Validation annotations on the DTO (TASK-022 will add `@Size` on the registration request, not on the entity) so the rules can move with UI changes without DB migrations. Avoiding CHECK constraints here also keeps room for future profile fields (longer display names, richer email handles) without a fragile schema rewrite.
- **No nullable `last_login_at` or `is_active` flag**: out of scope. The acceptance criteria literally lists "login, email, password hash, display name, and timestamps" — adding fields not asked for is the kind of scope creep CLAUDE.md warns against ("Don't add features beyond what the task requires"). Disabling/locking accounts and tracking last sign-in time are real concerns but they belong to dedicated future tasks with explicit UX (admin disables a user; reauth on suspicious activity).
- **Separate `created_at` and `updated_at` despite no trigger to maintain `updated_at`**: keeping both columns in the initial DDL avoids a follow-up migration when JPA/JPA's `@LastModifiedDate` lands in TASK-022's entity. The DEFAULT `now()` on `updated_at` covers the row's first write; subsequent writes are JPA's responsibility (Spring Data's `@EnableJpaAuditing` + `@LastModifiedDate` is the typical pattern). No DB trigger is necessary or desirable — keep the side-effects in the application layer where they're testable.
- **Named unique constraints (`users_login_unique`, `users_email_unique`) instead of inline `UNIQUE`**: predictable constraint names make integration tests assert on the violation error (`DataIntegrityViolationException` exposes the constraint name in its `cause.message` chain) without relying on Postgres' auto-generated `users_login_key`/`users_email_key` defaults. TASK-026's acceptance criteria explicitly says "rejects a duplicate login" — being able to assert "the constraint named `users_login_unique` fired" is more robust than parsing free-form error text.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **TASK-022 (User entity + repository)**: now has a real schema to map. Expected mapping is `@Entity @Table(name = "users")` on a `User` class in `com.trackify.user.domain`, with `@Id @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.UUID)` on the `id` field (Hibernate 6 supports UUID generation natively, no Postgres-side sequence needed). Audit timestamps land via Spring Data's `@CreatedDate` / `@LastModifiedDate` once `@EnableJpaAuditing` is added to a `@Configuration` class — not in scope for TASK-022 unless that task explicitly says so. JPA `ddl-auto: validate` (already set in `application.yml`) will fail the app boot if the entity diverges from the migration; this is the safety rail for the rest of the auth phase.
- **TASK-023 (Spring Session JDBC tables)**: the Spring Session JDBC schema lives in a separate, official `org.springframework.session/spring-session-jdbc` jar resource (`org/springframework/session/jdbc/schema-postgresql.sql`) — current `application.yml` has `spring.session.jdbc.initialize-schema: never` so TASK-023 must either copy that DDL into a `V3__spring_session.sql` migration (the cleaner path — keeps Flyway as the single schema authority) or flip `initialize-schema` to `embedded`/`always` (worse — splits schema ownership between Flyway and Spring Session). Recommend the migration-copy approach; the official schema is small (~30 lines) and stable across Spring Session versions.
- **TASK-026 (registration service) needs a `passwordEncoder` bean**: not yet wired anywhere. The `trackify.auth.password.encoder-strength: 10` property in `application.yml` is dangling — there's no `@ConfigurationProperties` class or `BCryptPasswordEncoder` bean reading it. TASK-024/025/026 will own that wiring; for now the schema is ready for whatever bcrypt cost factor lands.
- **No bootstrap admin user inserted by V2**: `application.yml` has `trackify.auth.bootstrap.admin.{login,email,password}` placeholders that, if set in `.env`, should auto-seed an initial user on first boot. Implementing that seed is a runtime concern (an `ApplicationRunner` in `auth.application`), not a migration concern. V2 leaves the table empty; the seed task (likely part of TASK-024 or TASK-025) inserts one row using the bcrypt encoder.
- **Database state warning**: this iteration applied V2 to the running `trackify-db` container directly. The next iteration that runs `./mvnw spring-boot:run` will see V2 already in `flyway_schema_history` with `success=t` and skip it cleanly — no double-application risk. If the database volume is ever wiped (`docker compose down -v`), Flyway in the next backend startup will replay V1 + V2 from scratch in order; verified the DDL is idempotent-safe in that sense (it does not depend on any prior `users` row or extension beyond what V1 enables).
- **No `package.json`/`pom.xml` change**, no new dependencies, no Java code changes. Frontend untouched. `tasks.json` change limited to flipping TASK-021 status to `done`.
- **Next pending task: TASK-022** (`User` entity + repository in `com.trackify.user`). The package skeleton is already there from TASK-009 (just `package-info.java` files); TASK-022 adds the first real Java file to the `user` module and validates `./mvnw compile`. With `ddl-auto: validate` in effect, the entity must align with this migration's column types/names exactly — any divergence will fail boot.


================================================================================
TASK-022 — Add the User entity and repository
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `backend/src/main/java/com/trackify/user/domain/User.java`: new JPA entity. Maps the `users` table created by V2 migration. Fields: `id` (UUID, @Id @GeneratedValue(strategy=UUID)), `login`/`email`/`passwordHash`/`displayName` (String, NOT NULL), `createdAt`/`updatedAt` (Instant, NOT NULL). Protected no-arg constructor for JPA. Public constructor takes the four user-supplied fields; id is filled by Hibernate, timestamps by entity lifecycle hooks (see below). Getters for all fields; setters only on the four user-mutable fields (no setter on `id`, `createdAt`, or `updatedAt` — those are managed). No equals/hashCode override — default Object identity used (see Design choices).
- **`@PrePersist` / `@PreUpdate` lifecycle hooks** populate `createdAt` and `updatedAt` from `Instant.now()`. **Reviewer-flagged fix**: the initial draft left both timestamp fields null and relied on the DB `DEFAULT now()` to populate them — but Hibernate includes every mapped column in the generated INSERT (even when null), which bypasses the DB default and would have triggered a NOT NULL violation on the first `save()`. Lifecycle hooks set both fields in-memory before insert (and bump `updatedAt` on every update), so JPA always sends non-null values. The DB defaults remain as a safety net for non-JPA inserts (migrations, psql scripts).
- `backend/src/main/java/com/trackify/user/infrastructure/UserRepository.java`: new Spring Data JPA repository extending JpaRepository<User, UUID>. Exposes: `findByLogin(String)`, `findByEmail(String)` (both Optional<User>), `existsByLogin(String)`, `existsByEmail(String)`. These are the exact lookups TASK-026 (registration) and TASK-027 (login) will need; nothing else was added.

What was tested:
- `./mvnw compile`: BUILD SUCCESS, 47 source files compiled (includes new User.java and UserRepository.java), exit 0.
- `./mvnw test`: BUILD SUCCESS, 4/4 tests pass (HealthControllerTest x1, GlobalExceptionHandlerTest x3). No regressions. The ERROR-level stack trace in test output is expected — it is the GlobalExceptionHandler's intentional log for a test that provokes a 500, not a test failure.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **No equals/hashCode override**: JPA entities with UUID PKs have a classic pitfall — id is null until after the first flush, so any `equals` that uses id will incorrectly treat two new, equal-value instances as unequal pre-persist and then "become equal" post-persist, breaking HashSet semantics. The cleanest solution for an MVP where no `Set<User>` or map-keyed-by-user is in scope is to leave Object identity in place. If set membership ever matters, the safe pattern is `equals` based on non-nullable business key (login or email is unique and non-null from construction) rather than the mutable/nullable id.
- **`Instant` for timestamps** (not `OffsetDateTime` or `LocalDateTime`): `TIMESTAMPTZ` stores an absolute UTC point; `Instant` maps to it most directly via Hibernate 6's JDBC type mapping for `timestamp with time zone`. `OffsetDateTime` works too but carries a zone offset that Postgres normalizes to UTC anyway — `Instant` is simpler and avoids accidental offset-arithmetic bugs.
- **`@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.UUID)**: Hibernate 6 (shipped with Spring Boot 3.x) has first-class UUID generation — no sequence, no DB function call from the JPA side, no custom `@GenericGenerator`. The V2 migration's `DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()` remains as a DB-side safety net for rows inserted outside JPA (migrations, psql scripts), but Hibernate will typically assign the UUID before the insert statement fires.
- **`updatable = false` on `id` and `createdAt`**: prevents accidental overwrite via `merge`.
- **`@PrePersist`/`@PreUpdate` over `@EnableJpaAuditing` for `updatedAt`**: standard JPA lifecycle hooks live on the entity itself, need no `@Configuration` change, and run regardless of how the entity is saved. Spring Data auditing (`@CreatedDate`/`@LastModifiedDate` + `@EnableJpaAuditing`) is the more idiomatic choice once auditing config is wired more broadly (e.g., `@CreatedBy` to track which user touched a row), but for a single field on one entity the lifecycle-hook approach is simpler, has fewer moving parts, and avoids a Spring config change that's outside TASK-022's scope.
- **Repository in `infrastructure` package, not `domain`**: Spring Data repositories are infrastructure concerns (they generate SQL and talk to a database); domain code should not import them. Controllers and application services (TASK-026/027) will inject `UserRepository` directly until a persistence adapter layer is warranted.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **`ddl-auto: validate` will fail on app boot if entity diverges from migration**: intentional. The seven `users` columns (id, login, email, password_hash, display_name, created_at, updated_at) map 1-to-1 to the entity fields. Any future schema change must be a new Flyway migration, not an entity-only change.
- **Lifecycle hooks vs Spring auditing**: when later tasks need `@CreatedBy`/`@LastModifiedBy` (audit who-did-what fields), the lifecycle-hook approach used here will be replaced by `@EnableJpaAuditing` + `@CreatedDate`/`@LastModifiedDate`/`@CreatedBy`/`@LastModifiedBy`. Both approaches map to the same DB columns; migrating from one to the other is a non-breaking, in-place change. No risk of stale `updatedAt` in the meantime — the hooks fire on every save.
- **No `UserService`, no registration DTO, no Spring Security wiring**: those are TASK-026 and TASK-027. This task deliberately stops at the persistence layer.
- **`existsByLogin` / `existsByEmail`**: Spring Data derives these as `SELECT count(*) > 0 FROM users WHERE login = ?` — efficient, no entity hydration. Added now because they are the cheapest way for the registration service to reject duplicates before attempting an insert, avoiding a round-trip on constraint violation.
- **Reviewer note**: the lifecycle-hook fix was applied after a code-review pass flagged the original null-timestamp insert bug. Final compile + test run was re-executed after the fix; both succeeded. This is the kind of subtle JPA pitfall worth remembering — `@Column(nullable=false)` plus a NOT NULL DB column plus a DB DEFAULT does **not** automatically defer to the default when the entity field is null. Either set the field in code (constructor, lifecycle hook, or auditing) or set `insertable=false` on the column to force Hibernate to omit it from the INSERT.


================================================================================
TASK-023 — Add Spring Session JDBC tables and config
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `backend/src/main/resources/db/migration/V3__spring_session.sql`: new Flyway migration. DDL copied verbatim from the official `spring-session-jdbc-3.4.1.jar` resource at `org/springframework/session/jdbc/schema-postgresql.sql`. Creates `SPRING_SESSION` (7 columns, PK `SPRING_SESSION_PK`, three indexes `SPRING_SESSION_IX1` through `SPRING_SESSION_IX3`) and `SPRING_SESSION_ATTRIBUTES` (`BYTEA` attribute column, composite PK, FK with `ON DELETE CASCADE` back to `SPRING_SESSION`).
- `backend/src/main/resources/application.yml`: no changes needed. Already had `spring.session.store-type: ${SPRING_SESSION_STORE_TYPE:jdbc}` and `spring.session.jdbc.initialize-schema: never` and `spring.session.jdbc.table-name: SPRING_SESSION`. Config was already correct.
- No new Java configuration class added. Spring Boot auto-configuration wires `JdbcIndexedSessionRepository` from the `spring-session-jdbc` dependency + the two `application.yml` properties — no `@EnableJdbcHttpSession` required.

What was tested:
- `./mvnw -q compile` and `./mvnw -q test-compile`: both exit 0 (no output = success).
- `./mvnw test`: BUILD SUCCESS, 4/4 tests pass (`HealthControllerTest` x1, `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` x3). No regressions.
- App boot with Flyway: `./mvnw spring-boot:run` (background). Log line confirmed: `Migrating schema "public" to version "3 - spring session"`. App reached `Started TrackifyApplication in 1.503 seconds`.
- `docker exec trackify-db psql -U trackify -d trackify -c "\dt spring_session*"` returned both `spring_session` and `spring_session_attributes` in the `public` schema owned by `trackify`.
- `\d spring_session` confirmed: 7 columns (`primary_id CHAR(36)`, `session_id CHAR(36)`, `creation_time BIGINT`, `last_access_time BIGINT`, `max_inactive_interval INT`, `expiry_time BIGINT`, `principal_name VARCHAR(100)`); PK `spring_session_pk`; unique index `spring_session_ix1` on `session_id`; plain indexes `spring_session_ix2` (expiry_time) and `spring_session_ix3` (principal_name).

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **Migration-copy approach (not `initialize-schema: always`)**: keeps Flyway as the single schema authority. Splitting schema ownership between Flyway and Spring Session's built-in initializer creates ordering ambiguity (which runs first?) and would silently re-create tables after a volume wipe if Flyway runs first but Spring Session doesn't see its tables yet. One migration, one owner.
- **DDL verbatim from the jar**: avoids drift. If the Spring Session version ever bumps and the schema changes, the diff between V3 and a future V4 migration will be explicit and reviewed. Stripping the top-level comment block from the source file is harmless — the DDL is byte-for-byte identical.
- **No `@EnableJdbcHttpSession`**: Spring Boot auto-config (`SpringSessionJdbcAutoConfiguration`) activates when `spring-session-jdbc` is on the classpath and `spring.session.store-type=jdbc` is set. Adding `@EnableJdbcHttpSession` would override the auto-config and require manual property wiring — more code, same outcome, worse.
- **`SPRING_SESSION` (uppercase table name)**: `application.yml` already had `spring.session.jdbc.table-name: SPRING_SESSION` matching the DDL. Postgres folds unquoted identifiers to lowercase so both `SPRING_SESSION` and `spring_session` resolve to the same table; the explicit property aligns Spring Session's SQL generator with the migration's intent.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **TASK-024 (Spring Security filter chain)**: this is the next pending task. It will add `SecurityFilterChain`, permit `/api/health`, require auth on all other endpoints, and wire the session cookie settings already present in `application.yml`. The session tables are now ready — Spring Session will start persisting sessions the moment a login endpoint exists and sets a session attribute. Risk: if the Security config doesn't explicitly set `sessionManagement().sessionCreationPolicy(IF_REQUIRED)` (or the default), Spring Session may try to create session rows on every request including the health check. Recommend permitting `/api/health` without a session.
- **Cookie `secure: true` in local dev**: `application.yml` sets `SERVER_SERVLET_SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=true` by default. HTTPS is required for the browser to send a `Secure` cookie. Local dev typically runs HTTP on `:8080`/`:5173`. The `.env.example` should set `SERVER_SERVLET_SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=false` for local profile; TASK-024 or the environment setup task should verify this doesn't block local login testing.
- **No V1/V2 modifications**: both prior migrations are untouched. Flyway history in `trackify-db` now has rows for versions 1, 2, and 3 all with `success=t`.


================================================================================
TASK-024 — Configure the Spring Security filter chain
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `backend/src/main/java/com/trackify/config/SecurityConfig.java`: new `@Configuration` class. Defines a single `SecurityFilterChain` bean built from the injected `HttpSecurity` and the existing `CorsConfigurationSource` from `WebConfig` (TASK-010). Authorization rules in declaration order: `/api/health` and `/api/auth/**` permitAll; `/error` permitAll; everything else under `/api/**` authenticated; `anyRequest()` denyAll. Session creation policy: `IF_REQUIRED` (default — explicit so the intent is on the page). Form login, HTTP Basic, and the auto-registered `/logout` endpoint are all disabled (`AbstractHttpConfigurer::disable`) — this is a JSON API consumed by an SPA, not a server-rendered app. CSRF is disabled (see Design choices). Authentication entry point is `HttpStatusEntryPoint(HttpStatus.UNAUTHORIZED)` so unauthenticated requests to protected paths return a clean 401 instead of Spring Security's default behavior (which depends on which auth mechanism is registered).
- `backend/src/test/java/com/trackify/config/SecurityFilterChainTest.java`: new test using `@WebMvcTest(controllers = HealthController.class)` + `@Import({SecurityConfig.class, WebConfig.class})`. Two tests: `healthIsPublic()` — GET `/api/health` returns 200 anonymous; `protectedRouteReturnsUnauthorizedWhenAnonymous()` — GET `/api/me` returns 401 anonymous. Notably, `/api/me` is not yet implemented (TASK-028 owns it) but Spring Security's `AuthorizationFilter` runs ahead of the dispatcher servlet and emits 401 before route matching ever happens — so the test exercises the filter chain rule, not a controller method. Same `@TestPropertySource` block as `HealthControllerTest` to satisfy `@Value` injection in `WebConfig`.

What was tested:
- `./mvnw -q compile`: clean (no output = exit 0). 48 source files compiled.
- `./mvnw test`: BUILD SUCCESS. Tests run: 6, Failures: 0, Errors: 0, Skipped: 0. Breakdown: SecurityFilterChainTest x2 (new), HealthControllerTest x1, GlobalExceptionHandlerTest x3. The ERROR-level stack trace in the raw output is the GlobalExceptionHandler's expected log line from the test that intentionally provokes a 500 — not a failure. No regressions in pre-existing tests.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **CSRF disabled for the API**: the SPA + cookie pattern's CSRF defense in this app is the combination of (a) `SameSite=Lax` on the session cookie (already set in `application.yml`: `SERVER_SERVLET_SESSION_COOKIE_SAME_SITE:lax`), which blocks cross-site form submissions from initiating state-changing POSTs that carry the cookie, and (b) the `CorsConfigurationSource` from `WebConfig` restricting allowed origins to `FRONTEND_ORIGIN` only, blocking credentialed XHR from other origins. Spring Security's default CSRF token mechanism is built for HTML form POSTs and would require either a `CookieCsrfTokenRepository` + an `XSRF-TOKEN` header on every mutation from the SPA's `httpClient`, or a `CsrfTokenRequestAttributeHandler` for SPAs — both add real frontend complexity that is out of scope for the MVP. Architecture.md does not call out CSRF separately. The trade-off: if the SPA is ever served from a different origin under credentialed CORS (allow-credentials true with a wildcard origin) or `SameSite=None`, CSRF would need to be re-enabled before that change ships. Documented here so the next person sees the full picture.
- **`/api/auth/**` permitAll instead of just `/api/auth/login`**: the auth phase will land `/api/auth/login`, `/api/auth/register`, and (future) password-reset endpoints. Permitting the whole `/api/auth/**` prefix avoids touching `SecurityConfig` again for every subsequent auth endpoint while still keeping a clear, narrow public surface (no other prefix is permitAll). The login/register handlers themselves will validate inputs; the security filter chain only decides whether the request is allowed past the gate. `/api/logout` (TASK-029) is intentionally not under `/api/auth/**` — it requires an authenticated session to invalidate, so it falls under the default authenticated rule.
- **`/error` permitAll**: Spring Boot dispatches uncaught exceptions to the `/error` servlet path. If Security blocks `/error`, an unauthenticated request that triggers an exception in a permitted path could 401-loop on its own error page. Permitting `/error` lets the `GlobalExceptionHandler` (TASK-011) render the proper `ApiError` envelope. There is no information disclosure risk: the `GlobalExceptionHandler` controls the response body explicitly and never echoes server internals.
- **`anyRequest().denyAll()`**: explicit denial outside `/api/**` is safer than `permitAll` — Spring Boot does not serve any non-API content from this app (the SPA is a separate Vite-built static bundle on `FRONTEND_ORIGIN`), so denying everything else closes the door on accidental endpoint exposure (e.g., a future Spring Boot starter that auto-registers a debug controller). If we ever add server-rendered pages, an explicit rule for that prefix gets added — the current rule will tell us we forgot.
- **`HttpStatusEntryPoint(401)` instead of letting Spring pick a default**: with form login disabled and HTTP Basic disabled, Spring's default `AuthenticationEntryPoint` selection logic is unpredictable — versions of Spring Security have shipped with `Http403ForbiddenEntryPoint` as a fallback, or with one that emits a 401 + `WWW-Authenticate: Basic` header even when Basic is disabled. The explicit entry point pins the behavior: 401 with no `WWW-Authenticate` header, which matches what the SPA expects (no browser-driven Basic auth dialog).
- **Session creation policy `IF_REQUIRED`**: the default. Explicitly set so the intent is on the page. `STATELESS` would defeat session-cookie auth; `ALWAYS` would create a session for every health probe and waste DB rows in `SPRING_SESSION`; `IF_REQUIRED` creates a session only when a filter or a controller actually puts an attribute (the login handler in TASK-027 will trigger that on successful credential validation).
- **Form login / HTTP Basic / `/logout` all disabled**: keeps the active filter list to the minimum needed for cookie session auth. The login flow (TASK-027) will be a custom `@PostMapping("/api/auth/login")` controller that validates credentials and calls `SecurityContextHolder.getContext().setAuthentication(...)` + (via Spring Session) persists. Logout (TASK-029) is a custom `@PostMapping("/api/logout")` that calls `request.getSession().invalidate()`. Disabling Spring's auto-registered `/logout` filter avoids a path collision and keeps every public route under explicit control.
- **`SecurityConfig` lives in `com.trackify.config`, not `com.trackify.auth.config` or `com.trackify.common.security`**: the filter chain is a cross-cutting infrastructure concern that wires beans across the app (CORS source from `web`, future authentication providers from `auth`). It belongs with the other cross-cutting `@Configuration` classes (`WebConfig`, `JacksonConfig`, `OpenApiConfig`). The `auth` module owns authentication *logic* (UserDetailsService, login/logout controllers, password encoding) — TASK-025/026/027 land there. `common.security` is for shared helpers (current-user resolver, role-check predicates). Keeping the layers separate avoids a future merge where moving the filter chain into `auth` would force `auth` to depend on `WebConfig`'s CORS bean across module boundaries.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **TASK-025 (auth properties binding)**: the dangling `trackify.auth.password.{encoder-strength,min-length}` and `trackify.auth.bootstrap.admin.*` properties in `application.yml` still have no `@ConfigurationProperties` class. TASK-025 owns that. The filter chain itself does not need them — the `PasswordEncoder` bean (BCrypt with the configured strength) is consumed by the login controller and registration service, not by `SecurityFilterChain` directly.
- **TASK-026 (registration) and TASK-027 (login)**: these will introduce a `UserDetailsService` (or an equivalent custom auth provider) that loads `User` rows by login and verifies the bcrypt hash. `SecurityConfig` does not need a `DaoAuthenticationProvider` bean today — Spring Security 6 auto-discovers a `UserDetailsService` bean and wires `DaoAuthenticationProvider` automatically when the `AuthenticationManager` is requested. If the login controller uses `AuthenticationManager.authenticate(...)` (the typical pattern), the chain is complete with no extra config in `SecurityConfig`. If a custom flow bypasses `AuthenticationManager` (e.g., manual `passwordEncoder.matches(...)`), the `UserDetailsService` bean is just unused — no harm.
- **TASK-028 (`/api/me` controller)**: the placeholder probe in `SecurityFilterChainTest` is `/api/me`. TASK-028 will wire a real controller that returns the current user. The 401 assertion in the test will continue to hold for unauthenticated requests after TASK-028 lands — the `authenticated()` rule fires before dispatcher routing. The test will need a parallel `@WithMockUser` test (or session-cookie-based test) added in TASK-028 to prove the 200-with-payload case; that is TASK-028's scope.
- **`/api/auth/login` security blast radius**: permitting all `/api/auth/**` includes future endpoints. Each one must be safe to expose anonymously (login, register, request-password-reset, complete-password-reset). `/api/auth/logout` is *not* anonymous and is intentionally outside this prefix — keeping logout on `/api/logout` (per architecture.md §11) prevents accidental anonymous logout-amplification. If a future task adds an authenticated `/api/auth/...` endpoint, it must add an explicit `requestMatchers(...).authenticated()` rule before the `/api/auth/**` line, or the task moves the new endpoint to a different prefix. Easy to overlook; flagging here.
- **`secure: true` cookie + local dev still unverified**: the prior progress note flagged that `SERVER_SERVLET_SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE` defaults to `true` and HTTPS is required for the browser to attach the cookie. TASK-024 does not fix that — it only configures the filter chain. Local dev login testing in TASK-027/031 will require either an HTTPS dev proxy or `SERVER_SERVLET_SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=false` in `.env` for the local profile. This is a real foot-gun for the next iteration.
- **No `PasswordEncoder` bean here**: deliberately. Putting a `BCryptPasswordEncoder` bean in `SecurityConfig` would couple the security filter chain to the credential-hashing strategy. The encoder bean belongs in the auth module (TASK-025 or TASK-026) where the registration service lives. Adding it now would be scope creep; not adding it does not break anything because `AuthenticationManager` is not requested by anything yet.
- **Test depends on `WebConfig` being importable**: `SecurityFilterChainTest` imports `WebConfig` to satisfy the `CorsConfigurationSource` dependency. If `WebConfig` is ever renamed, split, or moved, the test import breaks immediately. The `@TestPropertySource` block duplicates the four CORS keys from `application.yml` because `@WebMvcTest` does not load `application.yml` automatically. If `WebConfig` grows new `@Value` keys in a future task, this test will fail to wire `WebConfig` and the breakage will surface fast — annoying but visible, which is the intended trade-off of keeping config in `WebConfig` instead of duplicating the CORS bean across tests.


================================================================================
TASK-025 — Add password auth configuration binding
Date: 2026-04-26
================================================================================

What changed:
- `backend/src/main/java/com/trackify/auth/application/AuthProperties.java`: new typed binding for `trackify.auth.*`, declared as a Java record annotated `@ConfigurationProperties("trackify.auth")`. Fields: `password` (nested `Password` record with `encoderStrength` + `minLength`) and `bootstrap` (nested `Bootstrap` -> `Admin` record with `login`, `email`, `password`). Maps 1:1 to the existing `application.yml` keys (`trackify.auth.password.encoder-strength`, `.min-length`, `.bootstrap.admin.{login,email,password}`) and to their `.env` counterparts (`AUTH_PASSWORD_ENCODER_STRENGTH`, `AUTH_PASSWORD_MIN_LENGTH`, `AUTH_BOOTSTRAP_ADMIN_*`). Lives in the `auth` module per architecture.md §7.2 (auth owns "login/password authentication configuration" and "password hashing").
- `backend/src/main/java/com/trackify/auth/application/AuthSecurityBeans.java`: new `@Configuration` class that activates the binding (`@EnableConfigurationProperties(AuthProperties.class)`) and exposes a single `PasswordEncoder` bean built from the configured BCrypt strength: `new BCryptPasswordEncoder(authProperties.password().encoderStrength())`. This is the "reference from security setup" the test step calls out — the auth module's security beans are the security setup that consumes the typed config. The previously-dangling `trackify.auth.password.encoder-strength` value is now load-bearing.
- `backend/src/test/java/com/trackify/auth/application/AuthSecurityBeansTest.java`: new focused test using `ApplicationContextRunner` (no `@SpringBootTest`, no Postgres, no Flyway). Spins up a tiny context with `PropertyPlaceholderAutoConfiguration` + `AuthSecurityBeans`, supplies override property values, and asserts (a) `AuthProperties` bean fields equal the supplied values, (b) the `PasswordEncoder` bean is a `BCryptPasswordEncoder`, (c) `encoder.matches("hunter2", encoder.encode("hunter2"))` is true and the inverse is false. Catches binding bugs and encoder regressions in <300 ms with zero external dependencies.

What was tested:
- `./mvnw -q compile`: clean (no output, exit 0). Two new source files compile against the existing `pom.xml` deps.
- `./mvnw test`: BUILD SUCCESS. Tests run: 7, Failures: 0, Errors: 0, Skipped: 0. New: `AuthSecurityBeansTest#bindsAuthPropertiesAndExposesBcryptEncoderWithConfiguredStrength`. Existing 6 tests (SecurityFilterChainTest x2, HealthControllerTest x1, GlobalExceptionHandlerTest x3) unchanged.
- The user prompt asked for `./gradlew -p backend compileJava compileTestJava` and `./gradlew -p backend test`. Project is Maven (no `gradlew`/`settings.gradle.kts`) per CLAUDE.md ("always use the wrapper `./mvnw`"); ran `./mvnw compile` + `./mvnw test` instead — equivalent steps. CLAUDE.md is the source of truth for build commands.

Design choices and trade-offs:
- **Record over POJO**: Spring Boot 3 binds records to `@ConfigurationProperties` natively. Removes boilerplate, forces immutability. Loss vs class: no field-level Bean Validation annotations on synthetic accessors. For an MVP with simple ints + strings driven by env vars, validation can land in consumers (TASK-026 registration can check `password.length() >= props.password().minLength()` directly). Adding `@Validated` + parameter-level constraints is a backwards-compatible upgrade later.
- **Nested `Bootstrap` -> `Admin`** matches existing YAML shape (`trackify.auth.bootstrap.admin.login`) and leaves room for future bootstrap profiles. Flattening would force a YAML rewrite later.
- **Empty-string vs null for unset bootstrap admin values**: `application.yml` defaults each to `""` (`${AUTH_BOOTSTRAP_ADMIN_LOGIN:}`), so the record receives empty strings rather than null in normal operation. The seed runner can treat blank as "no admin configured" with a single `if (admin.login().isBlank()) return;`. Not switching to `Optional<String>` because YAML doesn't bind empty strings to `Optional.empty()` and the consumer normalizes anyway.
- **`AuthSecurityBeans` lives in `com.trackify.auth.application`, not `com.trackify.config`**: architecture.md §7.2 puts password hashing in the `auth` domain. Putting `PasswordEncoder` in `com.trackify.config` would couple the cross-cutting filter chain to credential strategy — exactly what the TASK-024 progress note argued against. Keeping it in `auth.application` lets future TASK-026/027 services depend on a domain-local `PasswordEncoder`.
- **`@EnableConfigurationProperties` on `AuthSecurityBeans`, not on `TrackifyApplication`**: scoping activation to the consumer keeps the auth module self-contained and grep-able. Avoids scattering activations across the root class.
- **Bean is `PasswordEncoder` (interface), implementation is `BCryptPasswordEncoder`**: consumers depend on the interface; swapping to `Argon2PasswordEncoder` later is a one-line change. `DelegatingPasswordEncoder` is overkill for a single-algorithm MVP — easy to add later if migration support is needed.
- **Test uses `ApplicationContextRunner`, not `@SpringBootTest`**: full app context activates JPA, Flyway, Postgres — none of which are relevant to property binding. `ApplicationContextRunner` boots a tiny isolated context (~100 ms), runs assertions, cleans up. Canonical Spring Boot pattern for testing config-properties binding. Means `./mvnw test` runs full-green even when Postgres is not running.

Follow-up risk / notes:
- **`UserDetailsServiceAutoConfiguration` warning at app boot**: with the new `PasswordEncoder` bean but no `UserDetailsService` yet, Spring Boot still prints the "Using generated security password" line at startup. Harmless (the auto-generated default user has no path through the SecurityFilterChain because form login is disabled) but noisy. TASK-026 will introduce a custom `UserDetailsService` that loads from `UserRepository`, causing the auto-config to back off on its `@ConditionalOnMissingBean` guard.
- **TASK-026 (registration service)**: now has both ingredients — `UserRepository` (TASK-022) for persistence and `PasswordEncoder` for hashing. Inject both + `AuthProperties` to enforce `min-length` on registration requests. Use `existsByLogin(...)` to reject duplicates and `passwordEncoder.encode(...)` to hash before storing.
- **TASK-027 (login)**: Spring Security 6 auto-builds a `DaoAuthenticationProvider` once both `UserDetailsService` (lands in TASK-026/027) and `PasswordEncoder` beans exist. The login controller can then delegate to `AuthenticationManager.authenticate(...)`. Either pattern works; the bean is pre-positioned for both.
- **Bootstrap admin seed runner**: still unimplemented. Trigger: "if `AUTH_BOOTSTRAP_ADMIN_*` env vars are non-empty at first boot, insert a single admin row using `passwordEncoder.encode(props.bootstrap().admin().password())`." Runtime concern (an `ApplicationRunner` in `auth.application`) — likely lands in TASK-026 or as a TASK-031 smoke-test prerequisite. Out of TASK-025's scope.
- **Encoder strength default of 10**: matches Spring Security's BCrypt default. Reasonable for dev; prod may want 12+ at the cost of ~4x login latency per increment. Env-var override is the lever.
- **No DB or migration changes**: pure Java config. V2 + V3 unaffected. `flyway_schema_history` unchanged.
- **No frontend changes**: SPA does not consume these properties.
- **Reviewer note**: putting both `AuthProperties` and `AuthSecurityBeans` in `auth.application` (not `auth.infrastructure` or a new `auth.config`) is deliberate. Spring Boot conventions + CLAUDE.md module layering treat configuration that exposes domain-relevant beans (password encoder) as application-layer glue. `auth.infrastructure` is reserved for adapters that talk to external systems.
